---
title: "Performance: CPU"
description: How xcc-compiled code compares with clang's Objective-C and C++, with GCC's C++ and with Swift on the same programs, measured on twenty benchmarks across arm64 and x86-64.
---

The compiler is measured on twenty programs, each written four times: in the
xc language, in Objective-C with ARC, in C++ and in Swift, doing the same work
with the same algorithm and the same data. All are built with optimisation
(`-O3` for xc, Objective-C and C++, `-O` for Swift), every version prints a
checksum, and a run only counts if all four checksums agree. Every program, in
all four languages, is on the [benchmark sources](/compiler/benchmark-sources/)
page; each benchmark's name below links to its own. On x86-64 the C++ source
is built twice, with clang and with GCC (`g++ -O3`), on both targets, so each
carries a column for each C++ compiler.

Each figure is measured with the released 0.9.4 `xcc` on two machines:

- **arm64:** an Apple MacBook Pro with an M3 Max (12 performance and 4
  efficiency cores, a 40-core GPU, no SME matrix unit), macOS; Apple clang 17.0.0, GCC 16.2.0 and Swift 6.1.2;
- **x86-64:** an AMD Ryzen 9 9955HX (16 cores, 32 threads), Linux; GCC 15.2.0, clang 21.1.8 and Swift 6.1.3; its
  CPU frequency governor is `performance` (amd-pstate-epp) for the run.

Every compiler is given the machine's full instruction set: `-march=native`
(x86-64) or `-mcpu=native` (arm64) for clang and GCC, `-target-cpu native` for
Swift. xc needs no flag for that: with its default `-msimd=auto`, xcc builds
each vectorised function for SSE2, AVX2 and AVX-512 and the program picks one
when it starts. Nothing else is set: no link-time optimisation, no
profile-guided build, no `-ffast-math`, on any side.

Times are seconds for the timed region, the best of five runs, each run waiting
until the machine is otherwise idle.

## Summary

How much faster xc's code runs than each language's, as the geometric mean
over eighteen of the twenty-two benchmarks:

| xc compared with | arm64 (Apple M3 Max) | x86-64 (AMD Ryzen 9 9955HX) |
|---|---|---|
| Objective-C | **1.42× faster** | **1.28× faster** |
| C++ (clang) | **1.13× faster** | **1.04× faster** |
| C++ (GCC) | **1.29× faster** | **1.29× faster** |
| Swift | **1.48× faster** | **1.55× faster** |

On both targets xc is ahead of all of them.

Four benchmarks are kept out of those means. xcc recognises the loop nest of
`matrix_mul_f32` and replaces it with a matrix kernel of its own, which makes xc
tens to hundreds of times faster at that one operation; counted in, it would
dominate means that stand for ordinary code, and most programs do not multiply
matrices. Its figures are under [Matrix multiplies](#matrix-multiplies).
`method_call` makes a virtual call in a loop, which xcc turns into a class test and a direct call it can inline, so xc runs it 11.6× faster on arm64 and 66× faster on x86-64 than C++ (clang). The result is real, and it is in every table and chart below, but one ratio that large would move a mean of ordinary code by itself.
`int_muldiv_hoist` and `array_sum_hoist` measure whether a compiler notices that
an outer loop repeats the same work on every pass. xcc does, and runs both in
microseconds, so their ratios say nothing about ordinary code; the tables show
them as "repetition removed".

## Per benchmark

Each row is a benchmark, each dot one language: how much faster or slower xc
is than that language. Dots left of the centre line are benchmarks xc wins. Choose a
release above a chart to see how it stood then; the rows stay in 0.9.4's
order. On arm64 every release's xc was timed again on the Apple M3 Max with today's programs, and is set against the other languages as measured there for 0.9.4. On x86-64 releases before 0.65 were measured against Objective-C alone, apart from `matrix_mul_f32`, which was added later and measured against all three.

| [`arc_alloc`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#arc_alloc) | 1.13 | 1.68 | 1.10 | 1.13 | 2.40 | **1.03× slower** |
| [`mem_copy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#mem_copy) | 1.25 | 2.21 | 1.26 | 1.87 | 1.22 | **1.02× slower** |
| [`float_math`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#float_math) | 1.12 | 1.12 | 1.12 | 1.10 | 1.12 | **1.02× slower** |
| [`branch_mix`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#branch_mix) | 1.03 | 1.03 | 1.03 | 1.04 | 1.04 | **1.01× slower** |
| [`hash_mix`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#hash_mix) | 1.27 | 1.27 | 1.27 | 1.27 | 1.27 | **the same** |
| [`matrix_mul`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#matrix_mul) | 0.25 | 0.26 | 0.25 | 0.46 | 1.60 | **the same** |
| [`int_accum`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#int_accum) | 1.22 | 1.22 | 1.22 | 1.23 | 1.23 | **the same** |
| [`array_sum`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#array_sum) | 0.98 | 0.99 | 0.99 | 3.55 | 0.99 | **1.01× faster** |
| [`arc_array`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#arc_array) | 0.61 | 5.71 | 0.63 | 0.85 | 1.21 | **1.02× faster** |
| [`bit_ops`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#bit_ops) | 1.23 | 1.29 | 1.27 | 1.30 | 1.29 | **1.03× faster** |
| [`sieve`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#sieve) | 1.24 | 1.67 | 1.67 | 1.42 | 2.21 | **1.14× faster** |
| [`call_depth`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#call_depth) | 0.87 | 1.04 | 1.03 | 1.10 | 1.03 | **1.19× faster** |
| [`string_scan`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#string_scan) | 1.05 | 2.54 | 2.56 | 1.51 | 2.55 | **1.43× faster** |
| [`method_call`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#method_call) | 0.10 | 2.55 | 1.21 | 0.21 | 1.19 | **2.0× faster** |
| [`matrix_mul_f32`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#matrix_mul_f32) | 2.00 | 10.97 | 10.97 | 10.95 | 12.13 | **5.5× faster** |
| [`array_sum_hoist`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#array_sum_hoist) | < 0.001 | 0.97 | 0.98 | 3.55 | 0.97 | repetition removed |

### A Raspberry Pi 5 (arm64 Linux)

The same programs on a Raspberry Pi 5 Model B Rev 1.0 (Cortex-A76) under Raspberry Pi OS, from 0.89: xc cross-built with `-A arm64-linux`, the C++ built on the Pi by clang++ 19.1 and g++ 14.2, all at `-O3`. Seconds; the best of five runs.

| benchmark | xc | C++ (clang) | C++ (GCC) | xc vs the faster of the two |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`struct_copy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#struct_copy) | 2.58 | 1.83 | 2.45 | **1.40× slower** |
| [`sieve`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#sieve) | 4.25 | 4.59 | 3.06 | **1.39× slower** |
| [`sort_small`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#sort_small) | 3.50 | 3.25 | 2.97 | **1.18× slower** |
| [`array_map`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#array_map) | 4.86 | 4.33 | 10.20 | **1.12× slower** |
| [`arc_alloc`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#arc_alloc) | 2.14 | 1.97 | 1.97 | **1.09× slower** |
| [`arc_array`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#arc_array) | 1.80 | 1.76 | 2.61 | **1.03× slower** |
| [`float_math`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#float_math) | 1.41 | 1.39 | 1.71 | **1.01× slower** |
| [`hash_mix`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#hash_mix) | 1.35 | 1.34 | 1.35 | **the same** |
| [`poly_dispatch`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#poly_dispatch) | 1.94 | 2.39 | 1.94 | **the same** |
| [`int_accum`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#int_accum) | 2.05 | 2.05 | 2.05 | **the same** |
| [`int_muldiv`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#int_muldiv) | 5.46 | 5.46 | 6.64 | **the same** |
| [`call_depth`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#call_depth) | 3.19 | 3.21 | 4.16 | **1.01× faster** |
| [`matrix_mul`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#matrix_mul) | 1.23 | 1.32 | 1.35 | **1.08× faster** |
| [`matrix_mul_f32`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#matrix_mul_f32) | 3.78 | 17.84 | 4.23 | **1.12× faster** |
| [`mem_copy`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#mem_copy) | 3.28 | 3.68 | 4.41 | **1.12× faster** |
| [`branch_mix`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#branch_mix) | 1.80 | 2.16 | 2.14 | **1.19× faster** |
| [`array_sum`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#array_sum) | 3.02 | 3.79 | 8.37 | **1.26× faster** |
| [`bit_ops`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#bit_ops) | 2.18 | 2.95 | 2.88 | **1.32× faster** |
| [`string_scan`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#string_scan) | 3.42 | 9.55 | 7.99 | **2.3× faster** |
| [`method_call`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#method_call) | 0.36 | 2.67 | 2.67 | **7.3× faster** |
| [`int_muldiv_hoist`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#int_muldiv_hoist) | < 0.001 | 4.82 | 1.45 | repetition removed |
| [`array_sum_hoist`](/compiler/benchmark-sources/#array_sum_hoist) | < 0.001 | 3.05 | 5.98 | repetition removed |

Geometric mean of xc's times against each: **1.09× faster** than clang++, **1.24× faster** than g++.

The fastest results are where the runtime does the work: `string_scan`,
`method_call` and `struct_copy` are byte scanning, dynamic dispatch and
aggregate copies. The slowest show where xcc's code generation has most to
gain:

- **Vectorisation.** On arm64, `int_muldiv` and `float_math` are vectorised
  by both compilers, and clang's loops are tighter; `matrix_mul` has matched
  clang there since 0.73. On x86-64 `matrix_mul` is still behind, because
  clang keeps the second matrix's rows in registers across the outer loop.
- **Reference counting on x86-64.** `arc_array`, where C++ reads elements
  without retaining them.
- **x86-64 loops and calls.** `sieve` and `sort_small`, where clang's loops
  are still tighter (in 0.74 `sort_small` was 2.6× behind; 0.75 rotates its
  inner loop), and `call_depth`, where clang's call sequence is shorter.

## Matrix multiplies

A dense matrix multiply written as three plain loops, `C[i][j]` the sum over
`k` of `A[i][k] · B[k][j]` in `float` or `double`, runs as a kernel the
compiler writes itself (from 0.71). The results are the loops', to the last
bit, and `-fno-matmul` turns it off. The kernel depends on the machine:

- **Apple M4 and later (SME):** the multiply runs on the matrix unit. From
  0.72 it works on a 2×2 block of the unit's tiles at once, and under
  [`:goal(speed)`](/compiler/language/statements/#speed-or-accuracy-goal),
  which `matrix_mul_f32` uses, it leaves out a NaN check that only affects the
  bits of a NaN (the inputs have none).
- **Apple M1 to M3 (no SME):** the program finds no matrix unit when it
  starts and runs the loops, vectorised. The arm64 figures on this page, for
  every release, come from an M3 Max, so there `matrix_mul_f32` is the loop;
  the table below adds an M4 Max, where it is the matrix unit.
- **x86-64:** the widest vector unit the processor has (SSE2, AVX2 or
  AVX-512), chosen when the program starts; the Ryzen 9 9955HX picks AVX-512.

`matrix_mul_f32` measures it: a 128×128 `float` multiply, 10,000 times.
(`matrix_mul` multiplies `u32` values, which the matrix unit does not take, so
it stays a vectorised loop.)

| `matrix_mul_f32` | xc | Objective-C | C++ (clang) | C++ (GCC) | Swift |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| arm64, Apple M4 Max (SME) | 28 ms | 10.9 s | 9.7 s | 9.7 s | 9.5 s |
| xc is | | **390× faster** | **348× faster** | **349× faster** | **342× faster** |
| arm64, Apple M3 Max | 2.0 s | 11.0 s | 11.0 s | 10.9 s | 12.1 s |
| xc is | | **5.5× faster** | **5.5× faster** | **5.5× faster** | **6.1× faster** |
| x86-64, AMD Ryzen 9 9955HX | 234 ms | 8.4 s | 8.3 s | 822 ms | 8.0 s |
| xc is | | **36× faster** | **35× faster** | **3.5× faster** | **34× faster** |

## The GPU and the network

`par` blocks, which run on every CPU thread, on GPUs and across machines, have
pages of their own: [Performance: GPU](/compiler/benchmarks/gpu/) (including
plain loops made parallel by `--par-style=auto`) and
[Performance: networked](/compiler/benchmarks/network/). Plain C built by xcc,
against GCC and clang on one thread and with OpenMP on every thread, is on
[Performance: C](/compiler/benchmarks/c/).

## Release to release

How fast xc's code is in each release, relative to 0.62: above 1× is faster.
The thin lines are single benchmarks (labelled where they moved by more than
twelve percent), the thick line the geometric mean (over the benchmarks the means cover, as in the [summary](#summary)). Releases shown: 0.62, 0.63, 0.64, 0.65, 0.66, 0.7, 0.71, 0.72, 0.73, 0.74, 0.75, 0.8, 0.85, 0.86, 0.87, 0.87.3, 0.88, 0.89, 0.9, 0.9.1, 0.9.2, 0.9.3, 0.9.4. `arc_alloc`, `method_call` changed in
0.64's benchmark set and are left out of this history.

## What is being compared

**The compiler that ships.** The xc numbers come from the `xcc` in the download.

**The same work in every language.** Every version keeps its data where the xc
version keeps it (local arrays, not `static` ones, which clang optimises
differently) and leaves nothing a compiler can remove: an object that could be
put on the stack outlives its iteration, a call whose target could be resolved
at compile time takes its class at run time, and results are folded in so no
loop has a closed form. Where the original's point is reference-counted
objects, the C++ version uses `std::shared_ptr` and the Swift version a class,
so they pay for reference counting too.

**Different runtimes on the two targets.** The Objective-C column is Apple's
Foundation on arm64 and GNUstep with libobjc2 on x86-64, and Swift is 6.2 on the
Mac and 6.1 on Linux. These are different implementations, so a language's
times compare within a target and not across one. Swift on Linux is markedly
slower on `poly_dispatch` and `array_map` than on the Mac; the runs were
repeated on an idle machine and reproduce.

**Timed regions of about one second.** Each benchmark times its own inner loop
rather than the process, so start-up and data set-up are excluded.

**Alignment noise on x86-64.** xcc aligns every loop head on x86-64 to a 32-byte
boundary and the start of `.text` to 64 bytes, so an unrelated change elsewhere
cannot move a loop across a fetch boundary; see
[Optimisation](/compiler/usage/optimization/#per-target-settings).

## Reproducing

The sources are in `benchmark/src`, one `.xc`, `.m`, `.cpp` and `.swift` per
program, and the runner builds and times them all:

```
python3 benchmark/run.py --opt O3 --repeats 5
python3 benchmark/page.py
```

The x86-64 legs cross-build xc here and build the other languages on the
configured Linux host; without one the runner measures this machine only.
`--langs` measures some of the languages and adds them to the results already
there. Results land in `benchmark/<version>/results.json`, and `page.py` turns
them into this page.
